\section{Addition retraction and the surviving multiplicative
core}\label{addition-retraction-and-the-surviving-multiplicative-core}

24 September 2026. The user explicitly retracts tau+tau=tau and says
tau+tau has no meaning. Accordingly this expression is no longer a
defined source operation. This is stronger than changing its value.
Earlier complete-operation tables remain historical constructions; they
are not the current specification. No decision about every other
previously supplied mixed sum is inferred from this one retraction.

\subsection{R1. Exact dependency
correction}\label{r1.-exact-dependency-correction}

The equation-based identification of \{0,tau\} as the additive
idempotents no longer applies: tau+tau cannot be evaluated. The previous
statement that integer 1 is the unique multiplicative but not additive
idempotent likewise cannot be used as an intrinsic predicate on this
whole carrier. The old distributive-multiplier test at x=y=tau has an
undefined input sum. The basis-relation defect
{[}tau+tau{]}-{[}tau{]}-{[}tau{]} is no longer a relation of the source.
The earlier Boolean addition and repeated-tau-sum arguments are
withdrawn as descriptions of the current source.

The full congruence and quotient statements require a specified domain
of addition and an explicit notion of morphism for that domain. They
must not be carried from the old total algebra into the current partial
specification without reconstruction. This does not invalidate proofs
about the former explicitly defined total algebra; it changes which
algebra the user is asking to study.

\subsection{R2. Addition can exist without a nontrivial parity
assignment}\label{r2.-addition-can-exist-without-a-nontrivial-parity-assignment}

Consider the three-element set C=\{0,a,b\} with the complete operation
table:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
+ & 0 & a & b \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0 & 0 & a & b \\
a & a & b & 0 \\
b & b & 0 & a \\
\end{longtable}
}

This operation is associative and commutative, has identity 0, and every
element has an additive inverse. For a direct proof, take a permutation
sigma cycling three positions, so sigma cubed is the identity. Identify
0 with the identity permutation, a with sigma and b with sigma squared.
The displayed table is exactly composition of these three permutations.
Composition is associative; the three powers commute; sigma and sigma
squared are mutual inverses. These facts prove all the additive-group
axioms for the table without assigning even or odd states.

In fact no nontrivial ordinary two-state parity assignment is compatible
with these sums. Suppose the nonzero elements were assigned even or odd,
with the usual rule that two odd states sum to an even state and two
even states sum to an even state when the result is nonzero. If a were
odd, a+a=b would force b even, and b+b=a would then force a even, a
contradiction. Exchanging a and b gives the same contradiction for b
odd. Thus the only such assignment labels both nonzero elements even; no
odd state can occur. This proof does not assign parity to zero.

The example establishes the general mathematical point: existence of
addition does not entail a nontrivial even/odd distinction. It does not
define an addition on tau. The source retraction in R1 is respected
independently of this counterexample. C is not renamed using the user's
Z\_n layer notation.

\subsection{R3. The global unit and the requested multiplicative
quotient
survive}\label{r3.-the-global-unit-and-the-requested-multiplicative-quotient-survive}

Retain M=Z disjoint union \{tau\}, ordinary multiplication on integers,
and tau x=x tau=x. The integer zero is absorbing. Products associate by
removal of tau unit factors followed by integer associativity. Tau is
the unique global multiplicative unit. Its distinction from integer 1
follows from 1 tau=1 different from tau, so integer 1 is not a unit on
the whole carrier.

Let B\_times=\{0,tau\} carry only its specified multiplication: zero
absorbs, tau is the unit. No addition on tau is introduced. Define

\[
\chi:M\to B_\times,\quad\chi(\tau)=\tau,\quad\chi(n)=0
\quad(n\in\mathbb Z).
\tag{R3.1}
\]

A product equals tau exactly when both factors are tau. Every other
product is an integer. These two cases prove chi(xy)=chi(x)chi(y),
including zero and unit. Its exact fibres are

\[
\chi^{-1}(\tau)=\{\tau\},\qquad\chi^{-1}(0)=\mathbb Z.
\tag{R3.2}
\]

Thus the requested `it and only it maps to it; everything else maps to
zero' quotient remains valid multiplicatively and uses no tau addition.

The second map r:M -\textgreater{} (Z,multiplication,1), with r(tau)=1
and r(n)=n, is multiplicative by the same pairwise check. Pairing chi
and r gives the injective map

\[
\tau\mapsto(\tau,1),\qquad n\mapsto(0,n),
\tag{R3.3}
\]

whose image is (\{0\} times Z) union \{(tau,1)\}. Its inverse sends the
displayed pairs to their source elements. Componentwise multiplication
preserves the image and the inverse preserves multiplication. This
retains every integer together with the distinction of tau. It does not
use componentwise addition or assign a value to tau+tau.

\subsection{R4. Algebra-preserving holonomy can still be proved
trivial}\label{r4.-algebra-preserving-holonomy-can-still-be-proved-trivial}

Consider automorphisms of the multiplicative carrier M that also
preserve the ordinary integer addition. The integer part is the
complement of the unique global unit tau, so such a bijection maps the
integer part to itself. The addition requirement concerns integer pairs
only; it assigns no sum to a pair of taus.

Every such automorphism f is the identity. First f fixes tau, the unique
multiplicative unit, and 0, the unique multiplicative absorber. The
multiplicative idempotents are exactly 0,1,tau: the integer equation n
squared=n is n(n-1)=0, giving 0 or 1. Hence f fixes the remaining
idempotent 1 as well. Integer addition then fixes every positive integer
inductively. The only elements satisfying x squared=1 are integer 1 and
-1, so injectivity and f(1)=1 force f(-1)=-1. Multiplication fixes every
negative integer as (-1)n.~This exhausts M.

Therefore transport by isomorphisms preserving this multiplicative
carrier and its integer addition has identity loop transport. This is a
reconstructed proof of the holonomy conclusion that does not use the
retracted tau+tau law, the former congruence theorem, or the old
additive-idempotent characterization. Multiplicative-only transport can
still permute primes, as the explicit 3/5 interchange in GS1
demonstrates; that interchange fixes tau and preserves parity
availability but fails integer addition.

\subsection{R5. A global tau section and an integer subsheaf without tau
addition}\label{r5.-a-global-tau-section-and-an-integer-subsheaf-without-tau-addition}

For a topological space X, let M\_X(U) be the locally constant functions
U -\textgreater{} M, with pointwise multiplication. Compatible functions
glue uniquely and the glued function remains locally constant, proving
the sheaf property. Let I\_X(U) be the integer-valued locally constant
functions. This is a subsheaf, is a multiplicative ideal, and carries
its ordinary pointwise integer addition. It includes the zero section.
No addition on sections involving tau has been defined by this
construction.

The constant tau function is the unique global unit section: evaluating
any unit section at a point gives the unique unit of M. The restrictions
of these unit sections agree on all overlaps, so their globality follows
by the sheaf gluing axiom. At the empty open there is the unique empty
section, which does not identify distinct source elements on any
nonempty open.

The pointwise map chi\_X:M\_X -\textgreater{} (B\_times)\_X is
multiplicative, is onto by the literal inclusion of \{0,tau\}, and
satisfies chi\_X inverse of the constant tau section = \{that same tau
section\}; its zero fibre is I\_X(U). Pairing it with r pointwise
reconstructs each original section by R3.3. This proves the
global-section and `everything else' sheaf construction with no use of
tau+tau.

The pointed prime spectrum and the multiplicative structure-sheaf
calculations in GS5-GS8 likewise use the unchanged multiplication. The
properties of the closed point c=Z, its whole-space-only open
neighbourhood, global multiplicative sections, and the closed quotient
remain available. Any former Boolean-addition or total-algebra assertion
in those notes must instead be read with the correction R1. The prime
point c is an ideal; it is not the element tau.

\subsection{R6. Parity-bearing fractions and their exact quotient
map}\label{r6.-parity-bearing-fractions-and-their-exact-quotient-map}

The usual construction of nonzero rational numbers starts from pairs
(a,b) of nonzero integers. Those two integers have parity. This is a
fact about the input presentation. It is different from a compatible
parity assignment on the represented rational value. The following maps
retain the actual input data and prove their relation.

Let F be the set of such pairs and put (a,b) equivalent to (c,d) exactly
when ad=bc. The rational quotient map is q(a,b)=a/b. The
numerator/denominator parity record is the pair of their ordinary
even/odd states. This record is not constant on fibres of q:

\[
q(1,2)=q(2,4),\qquad
(\mathrm{odd},\mathrm{even})\ne(\mathrm{even},\mathrm{even}).
\tag{R6.1}
\]

More generally every input (a,b) is equivalent to (2a,2b), whose two
parity entries are even. Therefore any function of only the raw two
parity entries that is invariant under the fraction equivalence is
constant on the rational quotient: every quotient element admits that
same even/even pair of entries. This proves precisely why the raw labels
do not descend. It does not remove the labels from the original input
pair, and the pair remains retained in F.

A stronger observable does descend. For a nonzero integer a, let v\_2(a)
be the largest nonnegative integer j for which 2\^{}j divides a.
Existence follows because 2\^{}j \textless= \textbar a\textbar{}
whenever it divides a. Writing a=2\^{}j u with u an odd signed integer
proves v\_2(ac)=v\_2(a)+v\_2(c): the product of two odd integers is odd.
For the original pair define

\[
v(a,b)=v_2(a)-v_2(b).
\tag{R6.2}
\]

If ad=bc, taking integer valuations gives
v\_2(a)+v\_2(d)=v\_2(b)+v\_2(c), so the two differences agree. Thus
there is a well-defined observable v on nonzero rational values with
v(q(a,b))=v(a,b). No reduction of the fraction is required, and the full
pair remains available separately. Multiplication of pairs proves
v(xy)=v(x)+v(y).

For addition use the larger pair carrier F\_0=Z times (Z minus \{0\}),
so a zero numerator is also allowed. The quotient map q\_0:F\_0
-\textgreater{} Q sends (a,b) to a/b and restricts to q on F. The
addition below has domain F\_0 times F\_0 and codomain F\_0; its
denominator is nonzero because b and d are. Retain the complete formula

\[
(a,b)+(c,d)=(ad+bc,bd).
\tag{R6.3}
\]

This formula descends to the rational quotient. Indeed, if ab'=a'b and
cd'=c'd, then

\[
\begin{aligned}
(ad+bc)b'd'
&=ab'dd'+bb'cd'\\
&=a'bdd'+bb'c'd\\
&=(a'd'+b'c')bd.
\end{aligned}
\tag{R6.3a}
\]

These are exactly the cross-products asserting equivalence of the two
output presentations. The original presentations and their parity
records remain in F\_0; the quotient map identifies rational values
without asserting that their presentation records coincide.

When ad+bc is nonzero, its output observable is exactly
v\_2(ad+bc)-v\_2(bd). When the numerator is zero, the result is the zero
rational and lies outside the domain of this nonzero-value observable.
In particular x+x=2x for nonzero rational x gives

\[
v(x+x)=1+v(x),\qquad
v(1/2)=-1,\qquad v(1)=0.
\tag{R6.4}
\]

This retains an exact connection to the integer parity data: a nonzero
integer n is odd precisely when v(n)=0 and even precisely when
v(n)\textgreater=1. Nonzero rationals also admit negative v values. The
observable has not been relabeled as one of the user's Z\_n layers or
identified with a two-state grading.

The earlier half-plus-half argument correctly rules out a compatible
two-state parity on rational values extending integer parity. It does
not establish that no parity-bearing ingredients were used to construct
those values. The claim that every possible construction of addition
needs parity is a broader claim about allowed constructions; it does not
follow from the usual integer-pair presentation. R2 gives a complete
additive structure without a nontrivial compatible two-state grading,
while R6 records exactly the integer-derived data the user asks to
retain. Neither calculation defines tau+tau or restores its retracted
law.

\subsection{Current status}\label{current-status}

The active common core consists of multiplication on M, ordinary
addition on its integer part, and the displayed partial-parity domain on
nonzero integers. Previously written additional mixed sums are not
promoted to a new completion here. The user's latest explicit change
removes tau+tau from the domain. R2 disproves the requirement of a
compatible nontrivial two-state parity on the elements of every additive
structure. It does not assert that an arbitrary construction uses no
input with parity data. R6 proves the exact distinction for integer
fraction presentations. R3-R5 construct the requested global tau and
multiplicative quotient without defining the retracted sum.

\section{The fixed tau and integer torsion: construction from the
retained
source}\label{the-fixed-tau-and-integer-torsion-construction-from-the-retained-source}

24 September 2026. This proof starts with the user's primitive tau and
existing integers. It does not derive integer arithmetic from
presentness alone. It introduces no metric, midpoint, vector, pairing,
or tau addition. The exact user quotations are in
\nolinkurl{VERBATIM_USER_DEFINITIONS_AND_CORRECTIONS.md}; V01-V08 concern the
source, and V09-V11 direct continued work from that source. Integer
translations TRZ1-TRZ4 were read in full before this receiving
calculation. Their exact source is recorded below.

\subsection{F0. Supplied definitions, followed by derived
distinctions}\label{f0.-supplied-definitions-followed-by-derived-distinctions}

The supplied starting data are Z\_1 presentness, tau with no Z\_2 parity
(V16), existing integer arithmetic with its complete amounts (V03), and
the retained Z\_2 grading on nonzero integers (V17). The grading is part
of the structured elements, so equality preserves its availability and
value. The supplied multiplicative rules include tau\emph{1=1 and the
global identity declaration (V13-V14). Ordinary integer arithmetic
includes 0}1=0 and 1 different from 0. No statement of tau's
distinctness is added as an independent axiom.

First derive tau different from each nonzero integer n.~Such an n
carries Z\_2 parity; tau does not. Equality would preserve that retained
structure, giving a contradiction. Next derive tau different from zero:
if tau=0, substituting in tau\emph{1=1 and using integer multiplication
gives 1=tau}1=0*1=0, a contradiction. These cases prove tau is not any
integer, without assigning a new parity to zero.

The intrinsic integer-amount map has domain Z and value n at integer
n.~Since the preceding proof places tau outside Z, this map has no value
at tau. Thus the absence of an intrinsic integer amount at tau is a
consequence of that domain and the proved distinction, not a second
assumption used to prove the distinction. An independently added
comparison map, such as the earlier multiplicative r(tau)=1, is not this
intrinsic amount map.

Let S denote the part of the programme consisting of these integer
elements and tau. The presentation S=Z disjoint union \{tau\} now
records the proved distinction. This is the specified stage under study,
not an assertion that the user's higher Z\_n layers have no further
elements.

On integer inputs addition, subtraction and multiplication have their
usual integer values. Addition and subtraction have no input involving
tau in the latest source transcription. Multiplication on all S has the
supplied global identity law tau\emph{x=x}tau=x (V13-V14). The original
integer values, signs and Z\_n labels remain in S. The earlier
convention that integer zero has no intrinsic Z\_2 label is not changed
by an auxiliary parity calculation below.

On Z define T\_k(n)=n+k, for integer k. This uses already supplied
integer addition; it is not a formula tau+k and is not a distance from
tau. T\_1 represents the step between the existing integer amounts by
the supplied arithmetic; no identity with an unconstructed geometric
involution is presumed. The question is how these integer bijections
extend to S, with no geometric interpretation imposed.

\subsection{F1. Unique extension and derived
fixedness}\label{f1.-unique-extension-and-derived-fixedness}

Every bijection f:Z -\textgreater{} Z has exactly one bijection f\_hat:S
-\textgreater{} S whose restriction to Z is f.~To prove necessity, take
any such extension. The image of Z is all Z. If f\_hat(tau) were an
integer m, there would exist n in Z with f(n)=m. Injectivity would then
give tau=n, contradicting the distinction proved in F0 from the source
grading and multiplication laws. Thus f\_hat(tau) must be tau. Existence
follows by setting the value at tau to tau and using f on integers; the
resulting function is bijective, with inverse the same extension of f
inverse. This proves both assertions.

Applying this construction to T\_k gives a unique extension, written
T\_hat\_k. Fixedness of tau follows from the extension theorem, rather
than being a geometric midpoint premise. Its values, after this proof,
are

\[
\widehat T_k(\tau)=\tau,\qquad
\widehat T_k(n)=n+k\quad(n\in\mathbb Z).
\tag{F1.1}
\]

For k nonzero, no integer is fixed, because n+k=n would imply k=0 by
integer cancellation. Consequently

\[
\operatorname{Fix}(\widehat T_k)=\{\tau\}\quad(k\ne0),
\qquad \operatorname{Fix}(\widehat T_0)=S.
\tag{F1.2}
\]

This is the unique fixed element for these actual transformations. It
proves no distance or radius statement. The derivation uses the exact
source carrier through its integer layer; adding further source elements
would require a new extension calculation.

\subsection{F2. The full amount is retained by
composition}\label{f2.-the-full-amount-is-retained-by-composition}

For an integer n,

\[
(\widehat T_k\circ\widehat T_l)(n)=(n+l)+k=n+(k+l).
\]

Both sides fix tau by F1. Therefore, as functions on all S,

\[
\widehat T_k\circ\widehat T_l=\widehat T_{k+l},\quad
\widehat T_k^{-1}=\widehat T_{-k},\quad
\widehat T_0=\mathrm{id}_S.
\tag{F2.1}
\]

The map k -\textgreater{} T\_hat\_k is an isomorphism from the integer
additive group onto this transformation family. Surjectivity is the
definition of the family; injectivity follows by evaluation at integer
zero, T\_hat\_k(0)=k. Evaluation there is the inverse. It is not
evaluation at tau: evaluation at tau gives tau for every member, and
therefore cannot recover k.

In particular T\_hat\_1 composed with itself sends integer zero to 2 and
still fixes tau. The source stage S has retained the integer amount two.
Nothing has assigned two copies, two units of amount, or a changed
parity to tau itself. The source records the amount in the integer and
in its corresponding transformation. Opposite signs give inverse
transformations; they do not assign negative distances.

\subsection{F3. The two-state quotient and the retained integer
amount}\label{f3.-the-two-state-quotient-and-the-retained-integer-amount}

On integer amounts put n equivalent to m exactly when n-m=2j for some
integer j. This is the orbit relation of the subgroup consisting of
T\_\{2j\}: reflexivity uses j=0, symmetry uses -j, and transitivity adds
the two integer j values. Integer division by 2 writes each n uniquely
as 2j+r with r in \{0,1\}. Thus there are exactly two equivalence
classes, denoted E and O, containing 0 and 1 respectively.

Translation by one induces a well-defined bijection sigma on these
classes. Well-definedness follows because (n+1)-(m+1)=n-m. Its values
are sigma(E)=O and sigma(O)=E, since 2j+1 is in O and (2j+1)+1=2(j+1) is
in E. Hence sigma squared is the identity, while T\_1 squared is T\_2
and is not the identity on integers. The exact map rho:Z -\textgreater{}
\{E,O\} obeys

\[
\rho(T_1(n))=\sigma(\rho(n)),\qquad
\rho(T_2(n))=\rho(n),\qquad T_2(n)=n+2\ne n.
\tag{F3.1}
\]

The last inequality follows from 2 nonzero. These identities retain both
the original amount and the quotient; they do not substitute the two
classes for the integer source. The graph map n -\textgreater{}
(n,rho(n)) has inverse projection to its first coordinate, so recording
the class alongside the integer loses no integer information.

On nonzero integers E/O agrees exactly with the user's Z\_2 even/odd
labels. If intrinsic parity is undefined at zero under the retained Z\_0
convention, its compatibility with T\_1 has the exact domain Z minus
\{-1,0\}, so both input and output are nonzero. The integer orbit map
rho is a separate total map; its value at zero is not a change to that
intrinsic convention. No rho(tau) is defined. No cyclic group is
substituted for the user's Z\_2 notation.

\subsection{F4. Exact comparison with the previous rigidity
result}\label{f4.-exact-comparison-with-the-previous-rigidity-result}

The maps T\_hat\_k are bijections describing a changing integer amount.
They are not asserted to preserve all arithmetic operations. For integer
m,n,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\widehat T_k(m+n)&=m+n+k,\\
\widehat T_k(m)+\widehat T_k(n)&=m+n+2k,\\
\widehat T_k(mn)&=mn+k,\\
\widehat T_k(m)\widehat T_k(n)&=mn+k(m+n)+k^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{F4.1}
\]

The additive right-minus-left difference in the integer target is k; the
multiplicative right-minus-left difference is k(m+n)+k\^{}2-k. All terms
and domains are retained. On a product involving tau, the multiplicative
comparison instead agrees:
T\_hat\_k(tau\emph{n)=n+k=T\_hat\_k(tau)}T\_hat\_k(n). For tau*tau both
sides equal tau.

Thus the nontrivial translation family is compatible with the earlier
theorem R4 that the only automorphism preserving multiplication and
integer addition is the identity: the operation domains preserved by
these two transformation notions differ, and F4.1 is their exact
comparison. No conclusion about arbitrary analytic holonomy is inferred.

\subsection{F5. What is actually excluded by the source operation
language}\label{f5.-what-is-actually-excluded-by-the-source-operation-language}

In the partial algebra of F0, n-tau and tau+n are outside the respective
operation domains. There is no source distance symbol. Integer
expressions formed using integer constants and integer addition,
subtraction and multiplication remain integers by induction on the
expression: leaves are integers and every operation on integer inputs
has integer output. They cannot evaluate to tau by F0.

These statements prove an exclusion for the given source operations.
They neither introduce a positive scalar distance nor assert a universal
theorem forbidding added mathematical structures. The attachment's
unproved conversion from difference of structure to numerical positivity
has not been used anywhere in F0-F4. The construction here concerns the
actual retained integer amounts and the uniquely fixed tau.

rendered image was inspected, and the caption was moved clear of the map
